알고리즘 소개
\+ 왜 이 알고리즘이 푸는 문제가 R 고정에 I(M)/L(M) 최대화인지에 대한 논증

- 간단한 수식을 이용한 Top R과 Neuron Chunking의 비교 (목표가 다르다는 것)

아래처럼 하나의 흐름으로 묶는 것이 좋습니다. 알고리즘을 먼저 소개하고, \(R\)이 외부에서 정해진다는 사실로부터 Top-\(R\)과 Neuron Chunking의 목적 차이를 도출합니다.

````markdown
10. **Utility-guided chunk selection searches for a latency-efficient mask under a prescribed row budget.**

    A target sparsity `\rho` determines the number of weight rows to retain:
    ```math
    R=\left\lfloor(1-\rho)N\right\rfloor.
    ```
    Thus, the selection policy does not decide the sparsity level itself. It receives `R` as an externally prescribed budget and determines which rows should be loaded.

    Let `\mathcal G` be a set of contiguous candidate chunks. For each candidate `C\in\mathcal G`, define
    ```math
    I(C)=\sum_{i\in C}v_i,
    \qquad
    U(C)=\frac{I(C)}{T[|C|]}.
    ```
    Here `I(C)` is the activation importance retained by the chunk, while `T[|C|]` is its estimated flash-read latency.

    The algorithm sorts candidates by decreasing utility:
    ```math
    U(C_{(1)})
    \ge
    U(C_{(2)})
    \ge
    \cdots
    \ge
    U(C_{(K)}).
    ```
    Starting from an empty mask, it processes the candidates in this order. A candidate `C_{(j)}` is accepted when it does not overlap previously selected chunks and fits within the remaining row budget:
    ```math
    C_{(j)}
    \cap
    \bigcup_{D\in S}D
    =
    \varnothing,
    \qquad
    \sum_{D\in S}|D|+|C_{(j)}|\le R.
    ```
    Upon acceptance,
    ```math
    S\leftarrow S\cup\{C_{(j)}\}.
    ```
    The process continues toward the prescribed budget `R`, and the final mask is
    ```math
    M_i
    =
    \mathbf1
    \left\{
    i\in\bigcup_{C\in S}C
    \right\}.
    ```

    The motivation for the utility score follows from the additive latency model. For a mask decomposed into maximal contiguous chunks,
    ```math
    I(M)
    =
    \sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}I(C),
    ```
    while
    ```math
    \widehat L(M)
    =
    \sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}T[|C|].
    ```
    Therefore, the mask-level objective is
    ```math
    \frac{I(M)}{\widehat L(M)}
    =
    \frac{\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}I(C)}
         {\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}T[|C|]}.
    ```
    The candidate score
    ```math
    U(C)=\frac{I(C)}{T[|C|]}
    ```
    is the corresponding importance-per-latency ratio at the individual-chunk level. Sorting by `U(C)` is therefore a greedy heuristic for constructing a mask with a high global importance-per-latency ratio.

    The role of Neuron Chunking becomes clear by comparing it with conventional top-`R` sparsification. Under the same prescribed row count, top-`R` solves
    ```math
    \boxed{
    M_{\text{top-}R}
    \in
    \arg\max_{\substack{M\in\{0,1\}^{N}\\\|M\|_1=R}}
    I(M)
    }.
    ```
    Since
    ```math
    I(M)=\sum_i v_iM_i,
    ```
    top-`R` selects the `R` individually largest importance values. It maximizes retained importance without considering whether the corresponding weight rows are contiguous or scattered.

    Neuron Chunking instead targets
    ```math
    \boxed{
    M_{\text{chunk}}
    \approx
    \arg\max_{\substack{M\in\{0,1\}^{N}\\\|M\|_1=R}}
    \frac{I(M)}{\widehat L(M)}
    }.
    ```
    The symbol `\approx` indicates that the paper uses a greedy heuristic rather than an exact optimizer.

    The difference between the two policies is therefore
    ```math
    \boxed{
    \begin{aligned}
    \text{Top-}R
    &:\
    \max_{\|M\|_1=R} I(M),\\
    \text{Neuron Chunking}
    &:\
    \max_{\|M\|_1=R}
    \frac{I(M)}{\widehat L(M)}.
    \end{aligned}
    }
    ```

    Both policies start from the same externally determined row budget `R`. Their difference lies in what they optimize while selecting those rows:

    - Top-`R` asks which `R` rows preserve the greatest total importance.
    - Neuron Chunking asks which approximately `R` rows provide the greatest retained importance relative to their flash-read latency.

    Consequently, Neuron Chunking may replace scattered, individually high-importance rows with contiguous, slightly lower-importance rows when the resulting latency reduction is sufficiently large.

    Section 3.2.1 writes the formal constraint as
    ```math
    \|M\|_1\le R.
    ```
    Operationally, however, `R` acts as a target row count: it is determined by the assigned sparsity, and the greedy algorithm continues accepting chunks toward that budget. The fixed-`R` formulation above therefore captures the intended comparison with the budget-matched top-`R` baseline.
````

**# Idea for my bachelor's thesis:** *\*VLM in Flash\**

Seol Handong

**---**

**## Under the displayed upper-bound formulation, an exact optimum can always be represented by a single contiguous chunk.**

Let $N$ be the number of input channels of a weight matrix and let

$$M\in\\{0,1\\}^{N},$$

where $M\_i=1$ means that channel $i$ and its corresponding weight row are selected.

Let $\mathcal C(M)$ denote the set of maximal contiguous runs of selected channels. Under the paper's additive latency model,

$$\widehat L(M)=\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}T[|C|],$$

where $T[r]$ is the profiled flash-read latency associated with a contiguous chunk of $r$ rows.

Define the total retained activation importance as

$$I(M)=\sum\_{i=0}^{N-1}v\_iM\_i,$$

where $v\_i$ is the importance of channel $i$. The paper's displayed selection problem can then be written as

$$\boxed{\max\_{\substack{M\in\\{0,1\\}^{N}\\\1\le\\|M\\|\_1\le R}}\frac{I(M)}{\widehat L(M)}},\qquad R=\left\lfloor(1-\rho)N\right\rfloor.$$

The lower bound excludes the empty mask, for which the ratio is undefined.

For a contiguous chunk $C$, define

$$I(C)=\sum\_{i\in C}v\_i,\qquad U(C)=\frac{I(C)}{T[|C|]}.$$

Here $U(C)$ is the chunk's retained importance per unit of estimated flash-read latency.

Because distinct maximal chunks partition the selected channels,

$$I(M)=\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}I(C).$$

Therefore,

$$\frac{I(M)}{\widehat L(M)}=\frac{\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}I(C)}{\sum\_{D\in\mathcal C(M)}T[|D|]}=\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}\underbrace{\frac{T[|C|]}{\sum\_{D\in\mathcal C(M)}T[|D|]}}\_{w\_C}\underbrace{\frac{I(C)}{T[|C|]}}\_{U(C)}.$$

The coefficients satisfy

$$w\_C\ge0,\qquad \sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}w\_C=1.$$

Hence the mask-level importance–latency ratio is a latency-weighted average of the utilities of its constituent chunks:

$$\boxed{\frac{I(M)}{\widehat L(M)}\le\max\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}U(C)}.$$

Let $C^\star$ be the highest-utility contiguous interval whose length does not exceed $R$:

$$C^\star\in\arg\max\_{\substack{C\text{ contiguous}\\\1\le|C|\le R}}\frac{I(C)}{T[|C|]}.$$

The mask selecting only $C^\star$ is feasible and attains

$$\frac{I(C^\star)}{T[|C^\star|]}=U(C^\star).$$

Consequently,

$$\boxed{\max\_{\substack{M\in\\{0,1\\}^{N}\\\1\le\\|M\\|\_1\le R}}\frac{I(M)}{\widehat L(M)}=\max\_{\substack{C\text{ contiguous}\\\1\le|C|\le R}}U(C)}.$$

Thus, under the displayed objective and additive latency assumption, there always exists an exact optimum consisting of only one contiguous chunk. Multiple chunks can also be optimal in the exceptional case where every selected chunk has the same maximum utility.

This optimum can be found without examining all $2^N$ masks. Define the importance prefix sums

$$P\_0=0,\qquad P\_j=\sum\_{i=0}^{j-1}v\_i.$$

A chunk

$$C\_{i,\ell}=\\{i,i+1,\ldots,i+\ell-1\\}$$

then has

$$I(C\_{i,\ell})=P\_{i+\ell}-P\_i$$

and utility

$$U(i,\ell)=\frac{P\_{i+\ell}-P\_i}{T[\ell]}.$$

There are

$$\sum\_{\ell=1}^{R}(N-\ell+1)=\frac{R(2N-R+1)}2$$

contiguous intervals of length at most $R$. Without the length restriction, the total number is

$$\binom{N+1}{2}=\frac{N(N+1)}2.$$

Prefix sums require $O(N)$ preprocessing and make each interval score computable in $O(1)$. The exact optimum is therefore obtainable in

$$\boxed{O(NR)}$$

time and $O(N)$ memory, which becomes $O(N^2)$ time in the worst case $R=\Theta(N)$.

This simple exact solution exposes a mismatch between the displayed formulation and the implemented algorithm. The displayed constraint

$$\\|M\\|\_1\le R$$

permits the optimizer to leave most of the budget unused, whereas the paper's greedy procedure continues accepting chunks toward $R$. Its implemented behavior is therefore closer to a fixed-cardinality or cardinality-band problem such as

$$\\|M\\|\_1=R$$

or

$$R\_-\le\\|M\\|\_1\le R\_+,$$

for which the maximum-utility single-chunk argument no longer provides a general exact solution.



앞에 이게 있었다는 가정하에 아래를 약간 다듬어보렴

**## Utility-guided chunk selection searches for a latency-efficient mask under a prescribed row budget.**

A target sparsity $\rho$ determines the number of weight rows to retain:

$$R=\left\lfloor(1-\rho)N\right\rfloor.$$

Thus, the selection policy does not decide the sparsity level itself. It receives $R$ as an externally prescribed budget and determines which rows should be loaded.

Let $\mathcal G$ be a set of contiguous candidate chunks. For each candidate $C\in\mathcal G$, define

$$I(C)=\sum\_{i\in C}v\_i,\qquad U(C)=\frac{I(C)}{T[|C|]}.$$

Here $I(C)$ is the activation importance retained by the chunk, while $T[|C|]$ is its estimated flash-read latency.

The algorithm sorts candidates by decreasing utility:

$$U(C\_{(1)})\ge U(C\_{(2)})\ge\cdots\ge U(C\_{(K)}).$$

Starting from an empty mask, it processes the candidates in this order. A candidate $C\_{(j)}$ is accepted when it does not overlap previously selected chunks and fits within the remaining row budget:

$$C\_{(j)}\cap\bigcup\_{D\in S}D=\varnothing,\qquad \sum\_{D\in S}|D|+|C\_{(j)}|\le R.$$

Upon acceptance,

$$S\leftarrow S\cup\\{C\_{(j)}\\}.$$

The process continues toward the prescribed budget $R$, and the final mask is

$$M\_i=\mathbf1\left\\{i\in\bigcup\_{C\in S}C\right\\}.$$

The motivation for the utility score follows from the additive latency model. For a mask decomposed into maximal contiguous chunks,

$$I(M)=\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}I(C),$$

while

$$\widehat L(M)=\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}T[|C|].$$

Therefore, the mask-level objective is

$$\frac{I(M)}{\widehat L(M)}=\frac{\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}I(C)}{\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}T[|C|]}.$$

The candidate score

$$U(C)=\frac{I(C)}{T[|C|]}$$

is the corresponding importance-per-latency ratio at the individual-chunk level. Sorting by $U(C)$ is therefore a greedy heuristic for constructing a mask with a high global importance-per-latency ratio.

The role of Neuron Chunking becomes clear by comparing it with conventional top-$R$ sparsification. Under the same prescribed row count, top-$R$ solves

$$\boxed{M\_{\text{top-}R}\in\arg\max\_{\substack{M\in\\{0,1\\}^{N}\\\\\\|M\\|\_1=R}}I(M)}.$$

Since

$$I(M)=\sum\_i v\_iM\_i,$$

top-$R$ selects the $R$ individually largest importance values. It maximizes retained importance without considering whether the corresponding weight rows are contiguous or scattered.

Neuron Chunking instead targets

$$\boxed{M\_{\text{chunk}}\approx\arg\max\_{\substack{M\in\\{0,1\\}^{N}\\\\\\|M\\|\_1=R}}\frac{I(M)}{\widehat L(M)}}.$$

The symbol $\approx$ indicates that the paper uses a greedy heuristic rather than an exact optimizer.

The difference between the two policies is therefore

$$\boxed{\begin{aligned}\text{Top-}R&:\ \max\_{\\|M\\|\_1=R} I(M),\\\\\text{Neuron Chunking}&:\ \max\_{\\|M\\|\_1=R}\frac{I(M)}{\widehat L(M)}.\end{aligned}}$$

Both policies start from the same externally determined row budget $R$. Their difference lies in what they optimize while selecting those rows:

\- Top-$R$ asks which $R$ rows preserve the greatest total importance.

\- Neuron Chunking asks which approximately $R$ rows provide the greatest retained importance relative to their flash-read latency.

Consequently, Neuron Chunking may replace scattered, individually high-importance rows with contiguous, slightly lower-importance rows when the resulting latency reduction is sufficiently large.

Section 3.2.1 writes the formal constraint as

$$\\|M\\|\_1\le R.$$

Operationally, however, $R$ acts as a target row count: it is determined by the assigned sparsity, and the greedy algorithm continues accepting chunks toward that budget. The fixed-$R$ formulation above therefore captures the intended comparison with the budget-matched top-$R$ baseline.

앞 절에서 이미 \(I(M)\), \(\widehat L(M)\), \(U(C)\), 그리고 목적함수의 문제점을 정의했으므로 중복을 줄였습니다. 이어지는 부분은 **논문의 실제 알고리즘 → fixed-\(R\)로 해석하는 이유 → Top-\(R\)과의 차이** 순서가 자연스럽습니다.

````markdown
## The paper's greedy algorithm approximates a fixed-budget importance-per-latency objective.

The paper's implementation treats

$$R=\left\lfloor(1-\rho)N\right\rfloor$$

as a target number of weight rows to retain. Rather than deciding the sparsity level, the selection algorithm receives $R$ from the sparsity allocation and determines which rows should be loaded.

Let $\mathcal G$ denote the generated set of contiguous candidate chunks. Each candidate $C\in\mathcal G$ is assigned the utility

$$U(C)=\frac{I(C)}{T[|C|]},\qquad I(C)=\sum_{i\in C}v_i.$$

Thus, $U(C)$ measures the activation importance preserved by the chunk per unit of estimated flash-read latency.

The candidates are sorted by decreasing utility:

$$U(C_{(1)})\ge U(C_{(2)})\ge\cdots\ge U(C_{(K)}).$$

The algorithm initializes

$$S\leftarrow\varnothing,\qquad q\leftarrow0,$$

where $S$ is the set of accepted chunks and $q$ is the number of selected rows. It then processes the sorted candidates in order. A candidate $C_{(j)}$ is accepted if

$$C_{(j)}\cap\bigcup_{D\in S}D=\varnothing$$

and

$$q+|C_{(j)}|\le R.$$

Upon acceptance,

$$S\leftarrow S\cup\{C_{(j)}\},\qquad q\leftarrow q+|C_{(j)}|.$$

The procedure continues until $q=R$ or no remaining candidate can be accepted. The resulting mask is

$$\boxed{
M_i^{\mathrm{greedy}}
=
\mathbf1\left\{
i\in\bigcup_{C\in S}C
\right\}.
}$$

The algorithm may return fewer than $R$ rows when the remaining budget cannot be filled by any nonoverlapping candidate. Nevertheless, its operational goal is to select approximately $R$ rows rather than to determine the row count from scratch.

The candidate utility is the chunk-level counterpart of the mask-level objective

$$\frac{I(M)}{\widehat L(M)}
=
\frac{\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}I(C)}
{\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}T[|C|]}.$$

Accordingly, the algorithm gives priority to chunks with high importance per estimated latency and greedily combines them while attempting to fill the prescribed row budget. This is a heuristic construction: ranking chunks individually does not guarantee that their combination maximizes the global ratio.

The position of Neuron Chunking can be understood by comparing it with conventional top-$R$ sparsification. Given the same prescribed row count, top-$R$ selects

$$\boxed{
M_{\mathrm{top}\text{-}R}
\in
\arg\max_{\substack{M\in\{0,1\}^{N}\\\|M\|_1=R}}
I(M).
}$$

Because

$$I(M)=\sum_i v_iM_i,$$

this selects the $R$ channels with the largest individual importance values. It maximizes retained activation importance without considering the physical arrangement or flash-read latency of the selected rows.

Neuron Chunking instead aims to balance retained importance against flash latency:

$$\boxed{
M_{\mathrm{chunk}}
\approx
\arg\max_{\substack{M\in\{0,1\}^{N}\\\|M\|_1=R}}
\frac{I(M)}{\widehat L(M)}.
}$$

The symbol $\approx$ indicates that the paper's greedy procedure is intended as a fast heuristic for this objective, not as an exact optimizer.

The methodological difference is therefore

$$\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
\text{Top-}R
&:
\quad
\max_{\|M\|_1=R} I(M),\\
\text{Neuron Chunking}
&:
\quad
\max_{\|M\|_1=R}
\frac{I(M)}{\widehat L(M)}.
\end{aligned}
}$$

Top-$R$ always prefers the individually most important rows, even when they are scattered across storage. Neuron Chunking may instead select contiguous, slightly less important rows when the reduction in flash-read latency compensates for the lost importance.

Section 3.2.1 formally states the constraint as

$$\|M\|_1\le R.$$

The fixed-$R$ expression above is therefore not the literal displayed formulation. It is a reconstruction of the problem suggested by the algorithm and experimental comparison: $R$ is determined by the target sparsity, and the greedy procedure continues selecting chunks toward that prescribed budget.
````

이제 그 아이디어를 소개하는 파트를 작성하자

1. T[r]이 a + cr에 가깝다는 것 -> 그래프 개형과 R^2
2. L^(M) = aK(M) + cR(M)을 유도
3. T[r1 + r2] < T[r1] + T[r2]인 이유는 a가 c와 비교할 수 있을 정도로 크기 때문이라는 것.

아래 부분은 **논문의 주장**과 **우리가 공개 latency table에서 새로 관찰한 affine approximation**을 구분하여 작성했습니다. 특히 \(a\)와 \(c\)는 단위가 다르므로 직접 비교하지 않고, \(a/c\)를 사용하도록 수정했습니다.

````markdown
## The profiled chunk latency is closely approximated by an affine function.

The paper represents the latency of a contiguous chunk using the profiled lookup table

$$T[r],$$

where $r$ is the number of weight rows in the chunk. The released profiles store chunk size in kilobytes. Let $z$ denote the chunk size in KB. A least-squares fit to these profiles gives the affine approximation

$$\boxed{T_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)\approx a+c_{\mathrm{KB}}z.}$$

The fitted models are:

| Device | Profiled range | Affine fit, with \(z\) in KB and \(T\) in ms | \(R^2\) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Jetson Orin AGX | \(1\text{--}255\) KB | \(T(z)\approx0.01100+0.0001398z\) | \(0.9867\) |
| Jetson Orin Nano | \(1\text{--}350\) KB | \(T(z)\approx0.01573+0.0002842z\) | \(0.9940\) |

These $R^2$ values are not reported in the original paper; they are obtained by fitting the latency tables released with its implementation. The mean absolute percentage errors are approximately $4.22\%$ on AGX and $3.21\%$ on Nano.

The latency curves therefore have a nearly linear increasing shape with a positive intercept, together with relatively small local fluctuations. The approximation is especially useful as a structural model rather than as an exact replacement for every lookup-table entry.

The corresponding throughput is

$$\beta(z)=\frac{z}{T_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)}
\approx
\frac{z}{a+c_{\mathrm{KB}}z}.$$

Hence,

$$\beta(z)\longrightarrow\frac{1}{c_{\mathrm{KB}}}
\qquad\text{as}\qquad
z\longrightarrow\infty.$$

This produces the qualitative shape observed in the paper: throughput initially increases with contiguous-read size and then approaches a saturation level.

If one weight row occupies $b$ KB, a chunk containing $r$ rows occupies

$$z=br\text{ KB}.$$

Therefore,

$$T[r]
=
T_{\mathrm{KB}}(br)
\approx
a+c_{\mathrm{KB}}br.$$

Defining

$$c=bc_{\mathrm{KB}},$$

the row-based latency model becomes

$$\boxed{T[r]\approx a+cr.}$$

Here:

- $a$ is an effective fixed cost incurred once per contiguous chunk;
- $c$ is the marginal latency of adding one more row to that chunk.

The parameter $a$ should be interpreted as an effective per-chunk penalty that summarizes small-read inefficiencies. It is not separately measured by the paper as one particular hardware operation.

Let

$$K(M)=|\mathcal C(M)|$$

be the number of maximal contiguous chunks in mask $M$, and let

$$R(M)=\|M\|_1
=
\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}|C|$$

be the total number of selected rows. Substituting the affine approximation into the paper's additive latency model gives

$$
\begin{aligned}
\widehat L(M)
&=
\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}T[|C|]\\
&\approx
\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}
\left(a+c|C|\right)\\
&=
a|\mathcal C(M)|
+
c\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}|C|\\
&=
\boxed{aK(M)+cR(M)}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus, the latency depends only on two structural properties of the mask:

- the number of selected rows, $R(M)$;
- the number of separate contiguous chunks, $K(M)$.

When the row count is fixed at $R$,

$$R(M)=R,$$

the row-transfer term is constant:

$$\widehat L(M)\approx aK(M)+cR.$$

Consequently, differences in estimated latency among masks with the same row count arise from the number of chunks:

$$\boxed{
R(M)=R
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\min_M\widehat L(M)
\iff
\min_MK(M).
}$$

This explains why the paper emphasizes long contiguous chunks. A mask with fewer, longer chunks pays the effective fixed cost $a$ fewer times than a fragmented mask containing the same number of selected rows.

For two chunks of lengths $r_1$ and $r_2$, reading them separately costs

$$T[r_1]+T[r_2]
\approx
2a+c(r_1+r_2).$$

Reading the same rows as one contiguous chunk costs

$$T[r_1+r_2]
\approx
a+c(r_1+r_2).$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}
T[r_1]+T[r_2]-T[r_1+r_2]
&\approx a,
\end{aligned}
$$

and hence

$$\boxed{
T[r_1+r_2]
<
T[r_1]+T[r_2]
\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad
a>0.
}$$

The strict inequality itself requires only a positive intercept. Its practical significance depends on whether $a$ is large relative to the variable transfer cost $c(r_1+r_2)$:

$$
\frac{
T[r_1]+T[r_2]-T[r_1+r_2]
}{
T[r_1+r_2]
}
\approx
\frac{a}{a+c(r_1+r_2)}.
$$

Thus, it is not dimensionally meaningful to compare $a$ directly with $c$: $a$ has units of latency, whereas $c$ has units of latency per row. The relevant comparison is between $a$ and $cr$, or equivalently between the chunk length $r$ and the characteristic scale

$$\boxed{r_{\mathrm{fixed}}=\frac ac.}$$

Using the KB-based fitted models gives

$$\frac{a}{c_{\mathrm{KB}}}
\approx
\begin{cases}
78.7\text{ KB},&\text{AGX},\\
55.3\text{ KB},&\text{Nano}.
\end{cases}$$

The fixed per-chunk penalty is therefore comparable to the cost of transferring approximately $79$ KB on AGX and $55$ KB on Nano. This is large enough for fragmentation to have a substantial effect on small and medium-sized reads.

More generally, suppose two selected regions of lengths $r_1$ and $r_2$ are separated by an unselected gap of length $g$. Reading them separately costs

$$2a+c(r_1+r_2),$$

whereas loading the intervening gap and merging them into one read costs

$$a+c(r_1+r_2+g).$$

The merged read is faster precisely when

$$
a+c(r_1+r_2+g)
<
2a+c(r_1+r_2),
$$

or equivalently,

$$\boxed{g<\frac ac.}$$

Thus, under the affine model, it can be faster to load some additional neighboring rows if doing so eliminates a separate chunk. The ratio $a/c$ determines how large a gap can be bridged before the additional transfer cost exceeds the saved per-chunk cost.
````

*β*(*z*)⟶*c*KB​1​as*z*⟶∞.

잠깐. 논문에 throughput이 나와있다면 그걸로 비교할 수 있지 않겠니? 확인해보렴

맞습니다. 확인해 보니 **논문의 throughput 데이터와 직접 비교할 수 있고, affine model을 뒷받침하는 상당히 강한 결과가 나옵니다.**

공개 코드가 사용하는 throughput 계산식은

\[
\beta(z)
=
\frac{z/1024\ \mathrm{MiB}}
{T(z)/1000\ \mathrm{s}}
=
\boxed{\frac{1000z}{1024T(z)}}\quad\mathrm{MiB/s}
\]

입니다. \(T(z)\approx a+cz\)를 대입하면

\[
\boxed{
\widehat\beta(z)
=
\frac{1000z}{1024(a+cz)}
}
\]

가 됩니다.

## 실제 비교 결과

| Device | Latency-fit \(R^2\) | Throughput-fit \(R^2\) | \(z_{\mathrm{sat}}\)에서 실제 / 예측 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| AGX | 0.9867 | 0.9875 | \(5221/5238\) MiB/s |
| Nano | 0.9940 | 0.9866 | \(3096/2965\) MiB/s |

즉, affine latency model로부터 유도한 throughput curve도 실제 throughput을 잘 설명합니다.

- AGX 포화점 예측 오차: 약 \(0.33\%\)
- Nano 포화점 예측 오차: 약 \(4.24\%\)

여기서 포화 크기는 Appendix와 공개 profile에 반복되는

\[
z_{\mathrm{sat}}^{\mathrm{AGX}}=236\text{ KB},
\qquad
z_{\mathrm{sat}}^{\mathrm{Nano}}=348\text{ KB}
\]

를 사용했습니다.

## \(1/c\)와도 비교할 수 있지만 주의해야 한다

KB와 ms 단위를 반영하면 극한은 단순한 \(1/c\)가 아니라

\[
\boxed{
\beta_\infty
=
\lim_{z\to\infty}\widehat\beta(z)
=
\frac{1000}{1024c}
\quad\mathrm{MiB/s}
}
\]

입니다.

| Device | Affine-model 극한 | SSD advertised peak |
|---|---:|---:|
| AGX | \(6985\) MiB/s | \(7450\) MB/s \(=7105\) MiB/s |
| Nano | \(3436\) MiB/s | \(3500\) MB/s \(=3338\) MiB/s |

놀랍게도 affine slope가 암시하는 극한 throughput은 SSD의 advertised peak와 각각 약 \(1.7\%\), \(2.9\%\)밖에 차이가 나지 않습니다.

다만

\[
\beta_\infty=\frac{1000}{1024c}
\]

는 **회귀식을 무한한 청크 크기까지 외삽한 극한값**입니다. 논문이 \(236\) KB 또는 \(348\) KB에서 실제로 측정한 throughput과 동일한 값은 아닙니다. 따라서 다음 두 비교를 분리해야 합니다.

\[
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
\widehat\beta(z_{\mathrm{sat}})
&\leftrightarrow
\text{논문의 실제 profiled throughput},\\
\beta_\infty
&\leftrightarrow
\text{SSD의 장기적인 bandwidth ceiling}.
\end{aligned}
}
\]

논문 초안에는 다음처럼 넣으면 정확합니다.

````markdown
The affine latency model can also be validated in the throughput domain. The released implementation computes logical throughput as

$$
\beta(z)
=
\frac{1000z}{1024T(z)}
\quad\text{MiB/s},
$$

where $z$ is measured in KB and $T(z)$ in milliseconds. Substituting

$$T(z)\approx a+cz$$

gives

$$
\boxed{
\widehat\beta(z)
=
\frac{1000z}{1024(a+cz)}
}.
$$

This function increases with chunk size and approaches

$$
\boxed{
\beta_\infty
=
\frac{1000}{1024c}
\quad\text{MiB/s}
}.
$$

The resulting throughput curves closely match the released measurements. The coefficients of determination in the throughput domain are

$$
R^2_{\mathrm{AGX}}=0.9875,
\qquad
R^2_{\mathrm{Nano}}=0.9866.
$$

At the saturation sizes used by the released profiles,

$$
z_{\mathrm{sat}}^{\mathrm{AGX}}=236\text{ KB},
\qquad
z_{\mathrm{sat}}^{\mathrm{Nano}}=348\text{ KB},
$$

the measured and affine-predicted throughputs are approximately

$$
\begin{array}{c|cc}
&\text{Measured}&\text{Affine prediction}\\
\hline
\text{AGX}&5221\text{ MiB/s}&5238\text{ MiB/s}\\
\text{Nano}&3096\text{ MiB/s}&2965\text{ MiB/s}.
\end{array}
$$

The fitted asymptotic throughputs are

$$
\beta_\infty^{\mathrm{AGX}}
\approx6985\text{ MiB/s},
\qquad
\beta_\infty^{\mathrm{Nano}}
\approx3436\text{ MiB/s}.
$$

These values are also close to the advertised SSD limits of $7450$ MB/s and $3500$ MB/s, respectively. This comparison should be interpreted cautiously: $\beta_\infty$ is an extrapolated asymptote of the affine model, whereas the paper's plotted throughput is measured at finite chunk sizes.
````

따라서 단순히 “그래프가 선형처럼 보인다”보다 훨씬 강하게,

\[
\boxed{
T(z)\approx a+cz
\text{가 latency와 throughput 양쪽에서 실제 profile을 잘 설명한다}
}
\]

고 주장할 수 있습니다.

**## The profiled chunk latency is closely approximated by an affine function.**

The paper represents the latency of a contiguous chunk using the profiled lookup table

$$T[r],$$

where $r$ is the number of weight rows in the chunk. The released profiles store chunk size in kilobytes. Let $z$ denote the chunk size in KB. A least-squares fit to these profiles gives the affine approximation

$$\boxed{T\_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)\approx a+c\_{\mathrm{KB}}z.}$$

The fitted models are:

\| Device | Profiled range | Affine fit, with \\(z\\) in KB and \\(T\\) in ms | \\(R^2\\) |

\|---|---:|---:|---:|

\| Jetson Orin AGX | \\(1\text{--}255\\) KB | \\(T(z)\approx0.01100+0.0001398z\\) | \\(0.9867\\) |

\| Jetson Orin Nano | \\(1\text{--}350\\) KB | \\(T(z)\approx0.01573+0.0002842z\\) | \\(0.9940\\) |

These $R^2$ values are not reported in the original paper; they are obtained by fitting the latency tables released with its implementation. The mean absolute percentage errors are approximately $4.22\\%$ on AGX and $3.21\\%$ on Nano.

The latency curves therefore have a nearly linear increasing shape with a positive intercept, together with relatively small local fluctuations. The approximation is especially useful as a structural model rather than as an exact replacement for every lookup-table entry.

The corresponding throughput is

$$\beta(z)=\frac{z}{T\_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)}

\approx

\frac{z}{a+c\_{\mathrm{KB}}z}.$$

Hence,

$$\beta(z)\longrightarrow\frac{1}{c\_{\mathrm{KB}}}

\qquad\text{as}\qquad

z\longrightarrow\infty.$$

This produces the qualitative shape observed in the paper: throughput initially increases with contiguous-read size and then approaches a saturation level.

If one weight row occupies $b$ KB, a chunk containing $r$ rows occupies

$$z=br\text{ KB}.$$

Therefore,

$$T[r]

\=

T\_{\mathrm{KB}}(br)

\approx

a+c\_{\mathrm{KB}}br.$$

Defining

$$c=bc\_{\mathrm{KB}},$$

the row-based latency model becomes

$$\boxed{T[r]\approx a+cr.}$$

Here:

\- $a$ is an effective fixed cost incurred once per contiguous chunk;

\- $c$ is the marginal latency of adding one more row to that chunk.

The parameter $a$ should be interpreted as an effective per-chunk penalty that summarizes small-read inefficiencies. It is not separately measured by the paper as one particular hardware operation.

Let

$$K(M)=|\mathcal C(M)|$$

be the number of maximal contiguous chunks in mask $M$, and let

$$R(M)=\\|M\\|\_1

\=

\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}|C|$$

be the total number of selected rows. Substituting the affine approximation into the paper's additive latency model gives

$$

\begin{aligned}

\widehat L(M)

&=

\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}T[|C|]\\\\

&\approx

\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}

\left(a+c|C|\right)\\\\

&=

a|\mathcal C(M)|

\+

c\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}|C|\\\\

&=

\boxed{aK(M)+cR(M)}.

\end{aligned}

$$

Thus, the latency depends only on two structural properties of the mask:

\- the number of selected rows, $R(M)$;

\- the number of separate contiguous chunks, $K(M)$.

When the row count is fixed at $R$,

$$R(M)=R,$$

the row-transfer term is constant:

$$\widehat L(M)\approx aK(M)+cR.$$

Consequently, differences in estimated latency among masks with the same row count arise from the number of chunks:

$$\boxed{

R(M)=R

\quad\Longrightarrow\quad

\min\_M\widehat L(M)

\iff

\min\_MK(M).

}$$

This explains why the paper emphasizes long contiguous chunks. A mask with fewer, longer chunks pays the effective fixed cost $a$ fewer times than a fragmented mask containing the same number of selected rows.

For two chunks of lengths $r\_1$ and $r\_2$, reading them separately costs

$$T[r\_1]+T[r\_2]

\approx

2a+c(r\_1+r\_2).$$

Reading the same rows as one contiguous chunk costs

$$T[r\_1+r\_2]

\approx

a+c(r\_1+r\_2).$$

Therefore,

$$

\begin{aligned}

T[r\_1]+T[r\_2]-T[r\_1+r\_2]

&\approx a,

\end{aligned}

$$

and hence

$$\boxed{

T[r\_1+r\_2]

<

T[r\_1]+T[r\_2]

\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad

a>0.

}$$

The strict inequality itself requires only a positive intercept. Its practical significance depends on whether $a$ is large relative to the variable transfer cost $c(r\_1+r\_2)$:

$$

\frac{

T[r\_1]+T[r\_2]-T[r\_1+r\_2]

}{

T[r\_1+r\_2]

}

\approx

\frac{a}{a+c(r\_1+r\_2)}.

$$

Thus, it is not dimensionally meaningful to compare $a$ directly with $c$: $a$ has units of latency, whereas $c$ has units of latency per row. The relevant comparison is between $a$ and $cr$, or equivalently between the chunk length $r$ and the characteristic scale

$$\boxed{r\_{\mathrm{fixed}}=\frac ac.}$$

Using the KB-based fitted models gives

$$\frac{a}{c\_{\mathrm{KB}}}

\approx

\begin{cases}

78.7\text{ KB},&\text{AGX},\\\\

55.3\text{ KB},&\text{Nano}.

\end{cases}$$

The fixed per-chunk penalty is therefore comparable to the cost of transferring approximately $79$ KB on AGX and $55$ KB on Nano. This is large enough for fragmentation to have a substantial effect on small and medium-sized reads.

More generally, suppose two selected regions of lengths $r\_1$ and $r\_2$ are separated by an unselected gap of length $g$. Reading them separately costs

$$2a+c(r\_1+r\_2),$$

whereas loading the intervening gap and merging them into one read costs

$$a+c(r\_1+r\_2+g).$$

The merged read is faster precisely when

$$

a+c(r\_1+r\_2+g)

<

2a+c(r\_1+r\_2),

$$

or equivalently,

$$\boxed{g<\frac ac.}$$

Thus, under the affine model, it can be faster to load some additional neighboring rows if doing so eliminates a separate chunk. The ratio $a/c$ determines how large a gap can be bridged before the additional transfer cost exceeds the saved per-chunk cost.

여기서 불필요한 T[r1 + r2] < T[r1] + T[r2]를 덜어내고 (간결성을 해치니까)

대신 방금 니가 계산한

The affine latency model can also be validated in the throughput domain. The released implementation computes logical throughput as

$$
\beta(z)
\=
\frac{1000z}{1024T(z)}
\quad\text{MiB/s},
$$

where $z$ is measured in KB and $T(z)$ in milliseconds. Substituting

$$T(z)\approx a+cz$$

gives

$$
\boxed{
\widehat\beta(z)
\=
\frac{1000z}{1024(a+cz)}
}.
$$

This function increases with chunk size and approaches

$$
\boxed{
\beta\_\infty
\=
\frac{1000}{1024c}
\quad\text{MiB/s}
}.
$$

The resulting throughput curves closely match the released measurements. The coefficients of determination in the throughput domain are

$$
R^2\_{\mathrm{AGX}}=0.9875,
\qquad
R^2\_{\mathrm{Nano}}=0.9866.
$$

At the saturation sizes used by the released profiles,

$$
z\_{\mathrm{sat}}^{\mathrm{AGX}}=236\text{ KB},
\qquad
z\_{\mathrm{sat}}^{\mathrm{Nano}}=348\text{ KB},
$$

the measured and affine-predicted throughputs are approximately

$$
\begin{array}{c|cc}
&\text{Measured}&\text{Affine prediction}\\\\
\hline
\text{AGX}&5221\text{ MiB/s}&5238\text{ MiB/s}\\\\
\text{Nano}&3096\text{ MiB/s}&2965\text{ MiB/s}.
\end{array}
$$

The fitted asymptotic throughputs are

$$
\beta\_\infty^{\mathrm{AGX}}
\approx6985\text{ MiB/s},
\qquad
\beta\_\infty^{\mathrm{Nano}}
\approx3436\text{ MiB/s}.
$$

These values are also close to the advertised SSD limits of $7450$ MB/s and $3500$ MB/s, respectively. This comparison should be interpreted cautiously: $\beta\_\infty$ is an extrapolated asymptote of the affine model, whereas the paper's plotted throughput is measured at finite chunk sizes.

이걸 넣어라

아래처럼 구성하면 됩니다. 기존의 \(T[r_1+r_2]\) 및 \(a/c\), gap-bridging 논의는 모두 제거하고, throughput-domain 검증을 affine fitting 직후에 배치했습니다.

````markdown
## The profiled chunk latency is closely approximated by an affine function.

The paper represents the latency of a contiguous chunk using the profiled lookup table

$$T[r],$$

where $r$ is the number of weight rows in the chunk. The released profiles instead record chunk size in kilobytes. Let $z$ denote the chunk size in KB. A least-squares fit to these profiles gives

$$\boxed{
T_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)\approx a+c_{\mathrm{KB}}z.
}$$

The fitted models are:

| Device | Profiled range | Affine fit, with \(z\) in KB and \(T\) in ms | \(R^2\) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Jetson Orin AGX | \(1\text{--}255\) KB | \(T(z)\approx0.01100+0.0001398z\) | \(0.9867\) |
| Jetson Orin Nano | \(1\text{--}350\) KB | \(T(z)\approx0.01573+0.0002842z\) | \(0.9940\) |

These $R^2$ values are not reported in the original paper; they are obtained by fitting the latency tables released with its implementation. The mean absolute percentage errors are approximately $4.22\%$ on AGX and $3.21\%$ on Nano.

The latency curves therefore have a nearly linear increasing shape with a positive intercept, together with relatively small local fluctuations. The affine approximation is useful as a structural model rather than as an exact replacement for every lookup-table entry.

The affine model can also be validated in the throughput domain. The released implementation computes logical throughput as

$$
\beta(z)
=
\frac{1000z}{1024T_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)}
\quad\text{MiB/s},
$$

where $z$ is measured in KB and $T_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)$ in milliseconds. Substituting

$$T_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)\approx a+c_{\mathrm{KB}}z$$

gives

$$
\boxed{
\widehat\beta(z)
=
\frac{1000z}
{1024\left(a+c_{\mathrm{KB}}z\right)}
}.
$$

This function increases with chunk size and approaches

$$
\boxed{
\beta_\infty
=
\frac{1000}{1024c_{\mathrm{KB}}}
\quad\text{MiB/s}
}
$$

as $z\to\infty$. It therefore reproduces the qualitative behavior observed in the paper: throughput rises rapidly for small contiguous reads and gradually approaches a bandwidth ceiling.

The throughput curves derived from the affine model closely match the released measurements. The coefficients of determination in the throughput domain are

$$
R^2_{\mathrm{AGX}}=0.9875,
\qquad
R^2_{\mathrm{Nano}}=0.9866.
$$

At the saturation sizes reported in Appendices D and H,

$$
z_{\mathrm{sat}}^{\mathrm{AGX}}=236\text{ KB},
\qquad
z_{\mathrm{sat}}^{\mathrm{Nano}}=348\text{ KB},
$$

the measured and affine-predicted throughputs are approximately

$$
\begin{array}{c|cc}
&\text{Measured}&\text{Affine prediction}\\
\hline
\text{AGX}&5221\text{ MiB/s}&5238\text{ MiB/s}\\
\text{Nano}&3096\text{ MiB/s}&2965\text{ MiB/s}.
\end{array}
$$

The fitted asymptotic throughputs are

$$
\beta_\infty^{\mathrm{AGX}}
\approx6985\text{ MiB/s},
\qquad
\beta_\infty^{\mathrm{Nano}}
\approx3436\text{ MiB/s}.
$$

For comparison, the advertised SSD limits are

$$
7450\text{ MB/s}\approx7105\text{ MiB/s}
$$

for AGX and

$$
3500\text{ MB/s}\approx3338\text{ MiB/s}
$$

for Nano. The affine asymptotes differ from these advertised limits by approximately $1.7\%$ and $2.9\%$, respectively.

This comparison should be interpreted cautiously. The quantities $\beta_\infty$ are extrapolated asymptotes of fitted models, whereas the paper's throughput measurements are obtained at finite chunk sizes. Nevertheless, the agreement in both the latency and throughput domains supports the affine approximation over the profiled operating range.

To express the model in weight-row units, suppose one row occupies $b$ KB. A chunk containing $r$ rows then occupies

$$z=br\text{ KB}.$$

Therefore,

$$
T[r]
=
T_{\mathrm{KB}}(br)
\approx
a+c_{\mathrm{KB}}br.
$$

Defining

$$c=bc_{\mathrm{KB}},$$

the row-based latency model becomes

$$\boxed{
T[r]\approx a+cr.
}$$

Here:

- $a$ is an effective fixed cost incurred once per contiguous chunk;
- $c$ is the marginal latency of adding one more row to that chunk.

The parameter $a$ should be interpreted as an effective per-chunk penalty that summarizes small-read inefficiencies. It is not separately measured by the paper as one particular hardware operation.

Let

$$K(M)=|\mathcal C(M)|$$

be the number of maximal contiguous chunks in mask $M$, and let

$$
R(M)
=
\|M\|_1
=
\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}|C|
$$

be the total number of selected rows. Substituting the affine approximation into the paper's additive latency model gives

$$
\begin{aligned}
\widehat L(M)
&=
\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}T[|C|]\\
&\approx
\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}
\left(a+c|C|\right)\\
&=
a|\mathcal C(M)|
+
c\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}|C|\\
&=
\boxed{
aK(M)+cR(M)
}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus, the affine latency depends only on two structural properties of the mask:

- the number of selected rows, $R(M)$;
- the number of separate contiguous chunks, $K(M)$.

When the selected row count is fixed at $R$,

$$R(M)=R,$$

the row-transfer term is constant:

$$
\widehat L(M)
\approx
aK(M)+cR.
$$

For $a>0$, differences in affine latency among masks with the same row count are therefore determined entirely by their numbers of chunks:

$$
\boxed{
R(M)=R
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\min_M\widehat L(M)
\iff
\min_MK(M).
}
$$

This explains why the paper emphasizes long contiguous regions. Among masks containing the same number of rows, a mask with fewer and longer chunks pays the effective per-chunk cost $a$ fewer times than a fragmented mask.
````

여기서 R(M)이랑 L(M)을 바꿔보렴 각각 |C(M)|이랑 ||M||1으로
그게 더 자연스러운 듯 읽기에

맞습니다. 별도의 \(K(M)\), \(R(M)\)를 도입하지 않고 바로 \(|\mathcal C(M)|\), \(\|M\|_1\)을 쓰는 편이 더 자연스럽습니다. 아래 부분으로 교체하면 됩니다.

````markdown
Substituting the affine approximation into the paper's additive latency model gives

$$
\begin{aligned}
\widehat L(M)
&=
\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}T[|C|]\\
&\approx
\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}
\left(a+c|C|\right)\\
&=
a|\mathcal C(M)|
+
c\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}|C|\\
&=
\boxed{
a|\mathcal C(M)|+c\|M\|_1
}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus, the affine latency depends only on two structural properties of the mask:

- the number of separate contiguous chunks, $|\mathcal C(M)|$;
- the total number of selected rows, $\|M\|_1$.

When the selected row count is fixed at $R$,

$$\|M\|_1=R,$$

the row-transfer term is constant:

$$
\widehat L(M)
\approx
a|\mathcal C(M)|+cR.
$$

For $a>0$, differences in affine latency among masks with the same row count are therefore determined entirely by their numbers of chunks:

$$
\boxed{
\arg\min_{\substack{M\in\{0,1\}^N\\\|M\|_1=R}}
\widehat L(M)
=
\arg\min_{\substack{M\in\{0,1\}^N\\\|M\|_1=R}}
|\mathcal C(M)|
}.
$$

This explains why the paper emphasizes long contiguous regions. Among masks containing the same number of rows, a mask with fewer and longer chunks pays the effective per-chunk cost $a$ fewer times than a fragmented mask.
````

즉 최종 핵심식은

\[
\boxed{\widehat L(M)\approx a|\mathcal C(M)|+c\|M\|_1}
\]

이 됩니다. 별도의 보조기호 없이 마스크의 구조가 바로 드러나므로 더 읽기 좋습니다.

**## The profiled chunk latency is closely approximated by an affine function.**

The paper represents the latency of a contiguous chunk using the profiled lookup table

$$T[r],$$

where $r$ is the number of weight rows in the chunk. The released profiles instead record chunk size in kilobytes. Let $z$ denote the chunk size in KB. A least-squares fit to these profiles gives

$$\boxed{

T\_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)\approx a+c\_{\mathrm{KB}}z.

}$$

The fitted models are:

\| Device | Profiled range | Affine fit, with \\(z\\) in KB and \\(T\\) in ms | \\(R^2\\) |

\|---|---:|---:|---:|

\| Jetson Orin AGX | \\(1\text{--}255\\) KB | \\(T(z)\approx0.01100+0.0001398z\\) | \\(0.9867\\) |

\| Jetson Orin Nano | \\(1\text{--}350\\) KB | \\(T(z)\approx0.01573+0.0002842z\\) | \\(0.9940\\) |

These $R^2$ values are not reported in the original paper; they are obtained by fitting the latency tables released with its implementation. The mean absolute percentage errors are approximately $4.22\\%$ on AGX and $3.21\\%$ on Nano.

The latency curves therefore have a nearly linear increasing shape with a positive intercept, together with relatively small local fluctuations. The affine approximation is useful as a structural model rather than as an exact replacement for every lookup-table entry.

The affine model can also be validated in the throughput domain. The released implementation computes logical throughput as

$$

\beta(z)

\=

\frac{1000z}{1024T\_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)}

\quad\text{MiB/s},

$$

where $z$ is measured in KB and $T\_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)$ in milliseconds. Substituting

$$T\_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)\approx a+c\_{\mathrm{KB}}z$$

gives

$$

\boxed{

\widehat\beta(z)

\=

\frac{1000z}

{1024\left(a+c\_{\mathrm{KB}}z\right)}

}.

$$

This function increases with chunk size and approaches

$$

\boxed{

\beta\_\infty

\=

\frac{1000}{1024c\_{\mathrm{KB}}}

\quad\text{MiB/s}

}

$$

as $z\to\infty$. It therefore reproduces the qualitative behavior observed in the paper: throughput rises rapidly for small contiguous reads and gradually approaches a bandwidth ceiling.

The throughput curves derived from the affine model closely match the released measurements. The coefficients of determination in the throughput domain are

$$

R^2\_{\mathrm{AGX}}=0.9875,

\qquad

R^2\_{\mathrm{Nano}}=0.9866.

$$

At the saturation sizes reported in Appendices D and H,

$$

z\_{\mathrm{sat}}^{\mathrm{AGX}}=236\text{ KB},

\qquad

z\_{\mathrm{sat}}^{\mathrm{Nano}}=348\text{ KB},

$$

the measured and affine-predicted throughputs are approximately

$$

\begin{array}{c|cc}

&\text{Measured}&\text{Affine prediction}\\\\

\hline

\text{AGX}&5221\text{ MiB/s}&5238\text{ MiB/s}\\\\

\text{Nano}&3096\text{ MiB/s}&2965\text{ MiB/s}.

\end{array}

$$

The fitted asymptotic throughputs are

$$

\beta\_\infty^{\mathrm{AGX}}

\approx6985\text{ MiB/s},

\qquad

\beta\_\infty^{\mathrm{Nano}}

\approx3436\text{ MiB/s}.

$$

For comparison, the advertised SSD limits are

$$

7450\text{ MB/s}\approx7105\text{ MiB/s}

$$

for AGX and

$$

3500\text{ MB/s}\approx3338\text{ MiB/s}

$$

for Nano. The affine asymptotes differ from these advertised limits by approximately $1.7\\%$ and $2.9\\%$, respectively.

This comparison should be interpreted cautiously. The quantities $\beta\_\infty$ are extrapolated asymptotes of fitted models, whereas the paper's throughput measurements are obtained at finite chunk sizes. Nevertheless, the agreement in both the latency and throughput domains supports the affine approximation over the profiled operating range.

To express the model in weight-row units, suppose one row occupies $b$ KB. A chunk containing $r$ rows then occupies

$$z=br\text{ KB}.$$

Therefore,

$$

T[r]

\=

T\_{\mathrm{KB}}(br)

\approx

a+c\_{\mathrm{KB}}br.

$$

Defining

$$c=bc\_{\mathrm{KB}},$$

the row-based latency model becomes

$$\boxed{

T[r]\approx a+cr.

}$$

Here:

\- $a$ is an effective fixed cost incurred once per contiguous chunk;

\- $c$ is the marginal latency of adding one more row to that chunk.

The parameter $a$ should be interpreted as an effective per-chunk penalty that summarizes small-read inefficiencies. It is not separately measured by the paper as one particular hardware operation.

Let

$$K(M)=|\mathcal C(M)|$$

be the number of maximal contiguous chunks in mask $M$, and let

$$

R(M)

\=

\\|M\\|\_1

\=

\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}|C|

$$

be the total number of selected rows. Substituting the affine approximation into the paper's additive latency model gives

$$

\begin{aligned}

\widehat L(M)

&=

\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}T[|C|]\\\\

&\approx

\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}

\left(a+c|C|\right)\\\\

&=

a|\mathcal C(M)|

\+

c\sum\_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}|C|\\\\

&=

\boxed{

aK(M)+cR(M)

}.

\end{aligned}

$$

Thus, the affine latency depends only on two structural properties of the mask:

\- the number of selected rows, $R(M)$;

\- the number of separate contiguous chunks, $K(M)$.

When the selected row count is fixed at $R$,

$$R(M)=R,$$

the row-transfer term is constant:

$$

\widehat L(M)

\approx

aK(M)+cR.

$$

For $a>0$, differences in affine latency among masks with the same row count are therefore determined entirely by their numbers of chunks:

$$

\boxed{

R(M)=R

\quad\Longrightarrow\quad

\min\_M\widehat L(M)

\iff

\min\_MK(M).

}

$$

This explains why the paper emphasizes long contiguous regions. Among masks containing the same number of rows, a mask with fewer and longer chunks pays the effective per-chunk cost $a$ fewer times than a fragmented mask.

이걸 다시 작성해주렴

````markdown
## The profiled chunk latency is closely approximated by an affine function.

The paper represents the latency of reading a contiguous chunk using the profiled lookup table

$$
T[r],
$$

where $r$ is the number of weight rows in the chunk. The released profiles instead record chunk size in kilobytes. Let $z$ denote the chunk size in KB. A least-squares fit to the released latency tables gives

$$
\boxed{
T_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)\approx a+c_{\mathrm{KB}}z.
}
$$

The fitted models are:

| Device | Profiled range | Affine fit, with \(z\) in KB and \(T\) in ms | \(R^2\) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Jetson Orin AGX | \(1\text{--}255\) KB | \(T(z)\approx0.01100+0.0001398z\) | \(0.9867\) |
| Jetson Orin Nano | \(1\text{--}350\) KB | \(T(z)\approx0.01573+0.0002842z\) | \(0.9940\) |

These $R^2$ values are not reported in the original paper. They are obtained by fitting the latency tables released with its implementation. The mean absolute percentage errors are approximately $4.22\%$ on AGX and $3.21\%$ on Nano.

The latency curves therefore have an approximately linear increasing shape with a positive intercept and relatively small local fluctuations. The affine approximation is intended as a structural model rather than an exact replacement for every entry in the lookup table.

The model can also be validated in the throughput domain. The released implementation computes logical throughput as

$$
\beta(z)
=
\frac{1000z}{1024T_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)}
\quad\text{MiB/s},
$$

where $z$ is measured in KB and $T_{\mathrm{KB}}(z)$ in milliseconds. Substituting the affine latency model gives

$$
\boxed{
\widehat\beta(z)
=
\frac{1000z}
{1024\left(a+c_{\mathrm{KB}}z\right)}.
}
$$

This function increases with chunk size and satisfies

$$
\boxed{
\lim_{z\to\infty}\widehat\beta(z)
=
\frac{1000}{1024c_{\mathrm{KB}}}
\quad\text{MiB/s}.
}
$$

It therefore reproduces the qualitative behavior observed in the paper: throughput rises rapidly for small contiguous reads and gradually approaches a bandwidth ceiling.

The throughput curves derived from the affine model also closely match the released measurements. Their coefficients of determination are

$$
R^2_{\mathrm{AGX}}=0.9875,
\qquad
R^2_{\mathrm{Nano}}=0.9866.
$$

At the saturation sizes reported in Appendices D and H,

$$
z_{\mathrm{sat}}^{\mathrm{AGX}}=236\text{ KB},
\qquad
z_{\mathrm{sat}}^{\mathrm{Nano}}=348\text{ KB},
$$

the measured and affine-predicted throughputs are approximately:

| Device | Measured throughput | Affine prediction |
|---|---:|---:|
| AGX | \(5221\) MiB/s | \(5238\) MiB/s |
| Nano | \(3096\) MiB/s | \(2965\) MiB/s |

The fitted asymptotic throughputs are

$$
\beta_\infty^{\mathrm{AGX}}
\approx6985\text{ MiB/s},
\qquad
\beta_\infty^{\mathrm{Nano}}
\approx3436\text{ MiB/s}.
$$

For comparison, the advertised SSD limits are

$$
7450\text{ MB/s}\approx7105\text{ MiB/s}
$$

for AGX and

$$
3500\text{ MB/s}\approx3338\text{ MiB/s}
$$

for Nano. The fitted asymptotes differ from these advertised limits by approximately $1.7\%$ and $2.9\%$, respectively.

The asymptotic comparison should be interpreted cautiously. The quantities $\beta_\infty$ are extrapolated limits of the fitted models, whereas the paper measures throughput only at finite chunk sizes. Nevertheless, the agreement in both the latency and throughput domains supports the affine approximation over the profiled operating range.

To express the model in weight-row units, suppose one weight row occupies $b$ KB. A chunk containing $r$ rows then occupies

$$
z=br\text{ KB}.
$$

Therefore,

$$
T[r]
=
T_{\mathrm{KB}}(br)
\approx
a+c_{\mathrm{KB}}br.
$$

Defining

$$
c=bc_{\mathrm{KB}},
$$

the row-based latency model becomes

$$
\boxed{
T[r]\approx a+cr.
}
$$

Here:

- $a$ is the effective fixed latency incurred once per contiguous chunk;
- $c$ is the marginal latency of adding one more weight row to that chunk.

The parameter $a$ summarizes the effective penalty associated with small independent reads. It is inferred from the affine fit rather than separately measured by the paper as the cost of one particular hardware operation.

Substituting the affine approximation into the paper's additive mask-level latency model gives

$$
\begin{aligned}
\widehat L(M)
&=
\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}T[|C|]\\
&\approx
\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}
\left(a+c|C|\right)\\
&=
a|\mathcal C(M)|
+
c\sum_{C\in\mathcal C(M)}|C|\\
&=
\boxed{
a|\mathcal C(M)|+c\|M\|_1
}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus, under the affine approximation, mask latency depends only on

- the number of separate contiguous chunks, $|\mathcal C(M)|$;
- the total number of selected rows, $\|M\|_1$.

When the number of selected rows is fixed at $R$,

$$
\|M\|_1=R,
$$

the row-transfer term becomes constant:

$$
\boxed{
\widehat L(M)
\approx
a|\mathcal C(M)|+cR.
}
$$

For $a>0$, masks containing the same number of rows are therefore ordered by their numbers of chunks:

$$
\boxed{
\|M_1\|_1=\|M_2\|_1=R
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\widehat L(M_1)<\widehat L(M_2)
\iff
|\mathcal C(M_1)|<|\mathcal C(M_2)|.
}
$$

Equivalently,

$$
\boxed{
\arg\min_{\substack{M\in\{0,1\}^N\\\|M\|_1=R}}
\widehat L(M)
=
\arg\min_{\substack{M\in\{0,1\}^N\\\|M\|_1=R}}
|\mathcal C(M)|.
}
$$

This explains why the paper emphasizes long contiguous regions. Among masks containing the same number of rows, a mask with fewer and longer chunks pays the effective per-chunk cost $a$ fewer times than a fragmented mask.
````

The parameter `a` should be interpreted as an effective per-chunk penalty that summarizes small-read inefficiencies. It is not separately measured by the paper as one particular hardware operation.

여기서 뭘 의미한 거니

여기서 \(a\)는 **실제로 특정 작업의 시간을 따로 재서 얻은 값이 아니라, 회귀직선의 절편**이라는 뜻입니다.

\[
T(z)\approx a+cz
\]

에서

- \(cz\): 데이터 크기에 비례하는 전송시간
- \(a\): 데이터 크기와 무관하게 청크 하나마다 추가되는 것처럼 보이는 시간

입니다. 예를 들어 AGX의 회귀식은

\[
T(z)\approx0.01100+0.0001398z\text{ ms}
\]

이므로

\[
a\approx0.011\text{ ms}=11\ \mu\mathrm{s}.
\]

하지만 이것이 “NVMe 명령 하나의 처리시간이 정확히 \(11\,\mu\mathrm{s}\)”라는 뜻은 아닙니다. 논문은 그런 특정 작업을 분리해서 측정하지 않았습니다.

\(a\)에는 다음과 같은 여러 효과가 합쳐져 있을 수 있습니다.

- I/O 요청 제출과 완료 처리
- 커널 및 direct-I/O 처리
- NVMe 명령과 인터럽트 처리
- SSD 컨트롤러 및 큐 관리
- 작은 읽기에서 발생하는 낮은 장치 활용률
- 블록 정렬 및 최소 읽기 단위의 영향
- 스레드 관리 비용

게다가 \(T(z)\)는 여러 요청을 실행한 steady-state 측정에서 얻었으므로, \(a\)를 단일 요청의 순수한 시작 비용으로 해석해서도 안 됩니다.

수학적으로 확실히 말할 수 있는 것은 이것입니다.

\[
\boxed{
a=\text{affine fit이 포착한, 청크 수가 하나 늘 때의 유효 추가비용}
}
\]

따라서 같은 양의 데이터를 \(k\)개 청크로 나누면

\[
\sum_{j=1}^kT(z_j)
\approx
ka+c\sum_{j=1}^kz_j
\]

가 되어, 청크가 하나 늘어날 때마다 모델상 약 \(a\)의 비용이 추가됩니다.

원문의 두 문장은 다음처럼 쓰는 것이 더 명확합니다.

> The intercept \(a\) is not the directly measured latency of a specific hardware operation. Rather, it is an aggregate parameter obtained from the affine fit: under this approximation, each additional chunk contributes approximately \(a\) units of latency beyond the size-proportional transfer cost.

즉, **\(a\)의 수학적 역할은 분명하지만 물리적으로 하나의 특정 원인에 대응한다고 단정할 수는 없다**는 뜻입니다.

Neuron Chunking instead aims to balance retained importance against flash latency:

$$\boxed{M_{\mathrm{chunk}}\approx\arg\max_{\substack{M\in\{0,1\}^{N}\\\|M\|_1=R}}\frac{I(M)}{\widehat L(M)}.}$$

The symbol $\approx$ indicates that the paper's greedy procedure is intended as a fast heuristic for this objective, not as an exact optimizer.


이제 여기서
이 문제에 대한 2^N이 아닌 해결책이 존재한다는 내용을 작성해라

아래 부분을 바로 이어 붙이면 됩니다. 핵심은 **마스크가 \(2^N\)개이지만, 청크 수는 인접한 두 비트만 보면 갱신할 수 있으므로 chain DP가 가능하다**는 것입니다.

````markdown
## The fixed-budget affine objective admits an exact polynomial-time dynamic program.

Substituting the affine latency model into the reconstructed fixed-budget objective gives

$$
\boxed{
\max_{\substack{M\in\{0,1\}^{N}\\\|M\|_1=R}}
\frac{I(M)}
{a|\mathcal C(M)|+cR}.
}
$$

Although there are $2^N$ possible masks, exhaustive enumeration is unnecessary. The selected channels form a one-dimensional binary sequence, and the number of chunks can be written as

$$
\boxed{
|\mathcal C(M)|
=
M_1+
\sum_{i=2}^{N}(1-M_{i-1})M_i.
}
$$

A new chunk begins exactly when a selected channel follows either the beginning of the sequence or an unselected channel. Consequently, the effect of selecting channel $i$ depends only on whether channel $i-1$ was selected. This local dependency allows dynamic programming over the channel chain.

For notational convenience, index the channel importances by $v_1,\ldots,v_N$. Define

$$
F_i^s(r,k)
$$

as the maximum importance obtainable from the first $i$ channels when

- exactly $r$ channels have been selected;
- exactly $k$ chunks have been formed;
- channel $i$ has selection state $s\in\{0,1\}$.

The initial conditions are

$$
F_0^0(0,0)=0,
$$

with every other initial state set to $-\infty$.

If channel $i$ is not selected, the previous sequence may end in either state:

$$
F_i^0(r,k)
=
\max
\left\{
F_{i-1}^0(r,k),
F_{i-1}^1(r,k)
\right\}.
$$

If channel $i$ is selected, it either continues an existing chunk or starts a new one:

$$
F_i^1(r,k)
=
v_i+
\max
\left\{
F_{i-1}^1(r-1,k),
F_{i-1}^0(r-1,k-1)
\right\}.
$$

The maximum importance achievable with exactly $R$ selected rows and $k$ chunks is therefore

$$
I^\star(R,k)
=
\max_{s\in\{0,1\}}F_N^s(R,k).
$$

The exact optimum of the affine fixed-budget problem is

$$
\boxed{
\max_{1\le k\le K_{\max}}
\frac{I^\star(R,k)}
{ak+cR},
}
$$

where

$$
K_{\max}
=
\min(R,N-R+1)
$$

is the largest possible number of chunks in a binary mask containing exactly $R$ selected positions.

The dynamic program has time complexity

$$
\boxed{
O(NRK_{\max})
}
$$

and rolling-array memory complexity

$$
O(RK_{\max}).
$$

Since $R\le N$ and $K_{\max}\le N$, this is polynomial rather than exponential, with a worst-case time complexity of $O(N^3)$. It is suitable as an offline exact oracle for measuring the optimality gap of the paper's greedy algorithm.

A faster formulation eliminates the explicit chunk-count dimension through fractional programming. For a parameter $\eta\ge0$, define

$$
\Psi(\eta)
=
\max_{\substack{M\in\{0,1\}^{N}\\\|M\|_1=R}}
\left[
I(M)
-
\eta
\left(
a|\mathcal C(M)|+cR
\right)
\right].
$$

Let

$$
\eta^\star
=
\max_{\substack{M\in\{0,1\}^{N}\\\|M\|_1=R}}
\frac{I(M)}
{a|\mathcal C(M)|+cR}.
$$

Then

$$
\boxed{
\Psi(\eta)
\begin{cases}
>0,&\eta<\eta^\star,\\
=0,&\eta=\eta^\star,\\
<0,&\eta>\eta^\star.
\end{cases}
}
$$

For a fixed $\eta$, define $G_i^s(r;\eta)$ as the maximum value of

$$
I(M)-\eta a|\mathcal C(M)|
$$

over the first $i$ channels, with $r$ selected channels and final state $s$.

The chain-DP transitions are

$$
G_i^0(r;\eta)
=
\max
\left\{
G_{i-1}^0(r;\eta),
G_{i-1}^1(r;\eta)
\right\},
$$

and

$$
G_i^1(r;\eta)
=
v_i+
\max
\left\{
G_{i-1}^1(r-1;\eta),
G_{i-1}^0(r-1;\eta)-\eta a
\right\}.
$$

Continuing a selected run adds no new chunk cost, whereas changing from state $0$ to state $1$ starts a new chunk and incurs the penalty $\eta a$.

After processing all channels,

$$
\boxed{
\Psi(\eta)
=
\max_{s\in\{0,1\}}
G_N^s(R;\eta)
-
\eta cR.
}
$$

Dinkelbach's method repeatedly solves this parametric chain problem and updates

$$
\eta_{t+1}
=
\frac{I(M_t)}
{a|\mathcal C(M_t)|+cR},
$$

where $M_t$ maximizes the transformed objective at parameter $\eta_t$. The procedure terminates when

$$
\Psi(\eta_t)=0
$$

up to the desired numerical tolerance.

Each parametric chain-DP evaluation takes

$$
O(NR)
$$

time. If Dinkelbach's method performs $q$ iterations, the total running time is

$$
\boxed{
O(qNR),
}
$$

with $O(R)$ memory for the objective values, excluding the additional storage required to reconstruct the mask.

The three-dimensional dynamic program provides a direct polynomial-time exact oracle, while the parametric chain-DP removes the explicit chunk-count dimension and is potentially much faster in practice. Both methods are exact for the fitted affine objective

$$
\frac{I(M)}
{a|\mathcal C(M)|+cR},
$$

but not automatically for the paper's original lookup-table objective when $T[r]$ deviates from $a+cr$.
````

우리 R(M)이랑 K(M) 그냥 쓰기로 했음 표기를. 그걸 고려해서 다시 작성해보

Although there are `2N` possible masks, exhaustive enumeration is unnecessary. The selected channels form a one-dimensional binary sequence, and the number of chunks can be written as

```math
∣C(M)∣=M1+∑i=2N(1−Mi−1)Mi.
```

A new chunk begins exactly when a selected channel follows either the beginning of the sequence or an unselected channel. Consequently, the effect of selecting channel `i` depends only on whether channel `i−1` was selected. This local dependency allows dynamic programming over the channel chain.

이게 뭔 말이니

Although there are `2N` possible masks, exhaustive enumeration is unnecessary. The selected channels form a one-dimensional binary sequence, and the number of chunks can be written as

```math
∣C(M)∣=M1+∑i=2N(1−Mi−1)Mi.
```

A new chunk begins exactly when a selected channel follows either the beginning of the sequence or an unselected channel. Consequently, the effect of selecting channel `i` depends only on whether channel `i−1` was selected. This local dependency allows dynamic programming over the channel chain.

이게 무슨 뜻인지 한국어로 설명해줘.

여기서 핵심은 **청크의 개수가 현재 채널과 바로 앞 채널만 보고 계산된다는 것**입니다.

먼저 오타를 바로잡으면, 가능한 마스크는 `2N`개가 아니라

\[
2^N
\]

개이고, 식은 다음과 같습니다.

\[
K(M)=|\mathcal C(M)|
=
M_1+\sum_{i=2}^{N}(1-M_{i-1})M_i.
\]

여기서 \(M_i=1\)이면 \(i\)번째 채널을 선택하고, \(M_i=0\)이면 선택하지 않습니다.

예를 들어

\[
M=(1,1,0,1,0,1,1)
\]

이라면 선택된 구간은

\[
\{1,2\},\qquad \{4\},\qquad \{6,7\}
\]

이므로

\[
K(M)=3
\]

입니다.

왜 위 식으로 청크 수를 셀 수 있는지 보면:

- \(M_1=1\)이면 첫 번째 위치에서 청크 하나가 시작됩니다.
- \(i\ge2\)에서는 \(M_{i-1}=0\)이고 \(M_i=1\)일 때만 새로운 청크가 시작됩니다.

이를 나타내는 것이

\[
(1-M_{i-1})M_i
\]

입니다.

| \(M_{i-1}\) | \(M_i\) | 의미 | \((1-M_{i-1})M_i\) |
|---:|---:|---|---:|
| 0 | 0 | 계속 선택하지 않음 | 0 |
| 0 | 1 | 새로운 청크 시작 | 1 |
| 1 | 0 | 기존 청크 종료 | 0 |
| 1 | 1 | 기존 청크 계속 | 0 |

따라서 예시에서는

\[
\begin{aligned}
K(M)
&=M_1+\sum_{i=2}^{7}(1-M_{i-1})M_i\\
&=1+0+0+1+0+1+0\\
&=3.
\end{aligned}
\]

“local dependency”라는 말은 \(i\)번째 채널을 선택할 때 새로운 청크가 생기는지 판단하려면, 이전의 선택 전체를 알 필요 없이 **바로 앞의 \(M_{i-1}\)만 알면 된다**는 뜻입니다.

- \(M_{i-1}=1,\ M_i=1\): 기존 청크가 연장되므로 \(K(M)\)는 그대로
- \(M_{i-1}=0,\ M_i=1\): 새 청크가 시작되므로 \(K(M)\)가 1 증가

그래서 동적 계획법은 앞부분의 정확한 마스크 모양을 전부 저장할 필요 없이 다음 정보만 유지할 수 있습니다.

\[
(i,r,k,s)
\]

여기서

- \(i\): 지금까지 살펴본 채널 수
- \(r\): 선택한 채널 수
- \(k\): 만들어진 청크 수
- \(s=M_i\): 마지막 채널의 선택 여부

동일한 \((i,r,k,s)\)에 도달하는 수많은 마스크 중에서는 importance가 가장 큰 경우만 남기면 됩니다. 즉, \(2^N\)개의 마스크를 하나씩 검사하는 대신, 같은 상태에 해당하는 경우들을 하나로 압축하는 것입니다.

특히 affine latency model이

\[
\widehat L(M)\approx aK(M)+cR(M)
\]

이고 \(R(M)=R\)이 고정되어 있다면, 마스크의 미래 비용을 결정하는 데 과거의 구체적인 모양은 필요하지 않습니다. 지금까지 선택한 행의 수 \(r\), 청크 수 \(k\), 마지막 비트 \(s\)만 있으면 충분합니다. 이것이 이 문제에 chain dynamic programming을 적용할 수 있는 이유입니다.

그러니까 이 표기는, chunk의 모든 시작부 0->1로 바뀌거나 1th에서 시작하는 걸 세겟다는 거구나

맞습니다.

\[
K(M)=M_1+\sum_{i=2}^{N}(1-M_{i-1})M_i
\]

는 청크의 모든 시작점을 세는 식입니다.

- \(M_1\): 첫 번째 위치가 `1`이면 거기서 시작하는 청크를 셈
- \((1-M_{i-1})M_i\): \(i\ge2\)에서 `0→1`로 바뀌는 지점을 셈

예를 들어

\[
M=(1,1,0,1,1,0,1)
\]

에서는 청크 시작점이

\[
1,\quad4,\quad7
\]

이므로

\[
K(M)=3
\]

입니다. 즉,

\[
\boxed{\text{청크 수}=\text{첫 위치에서 시작하는 경우}+\text{모든 }0\to1\text{ 전환의 수}}
\]

입니다.

[Attached image: sediment://file_00000000c38c8209b0e337d203988871]
잠깐. 애초에 이 시점에서 지수 시간은 거의 날라간 거냐

거의 맞습니다. 다만 **식 하나만으로 지수 시간이 사라지는 것은 아니고**, 이 식이 동적 계획법에 필요한 국소 구조를 드러내면서 지수 탐색을 제거할 수 있게 됩니다.

\[
K(M)=M_1+\sum_{i=2}^{N}(1-M_{i-1})M_i
\]

에서 새 청크가 생기는지는 오직

\[
(M_{i-1},M_i)
\]

만 보면 알 수 있습니다. 이전의 마스크 전체를 기억할 필요가 없습니다.

따라서 앞의 \(i\)개 채널을 처리했을 때 다음 상태만 저장하면 됩니다.

\[
(i,r,k,s)
\]

- \(r\): 지금까지 선택한 채널 수
- \(k\): 지금까지 시작된 청크 수
- \(s\in\{0,1\}\): \(i\)번째 채널의 선택 여부

같은 \((i,r,k,s)\)에 도달한 수많은 마스크 중에서는 \(I(M)\)가 가장 큰 것 하나만 남길 수 있습니다. 예를 들어 수백만 개의 서로 다른 마스크가 이후의 관점에서는 동일한 상태로 압축될 수 있습니다.

특히

\[
\widehat L(M)\approx aK(M)+cR(M)
\]

이고

\[
R(M)=R
\]

이 고정되어 있다면, 각 \(k\)에 대해

\[
I^\star(R,k)
=
\max_{\substack{R(M)=R\\K(M)=k}}I(M)
\]

를 DP로 구한 다음,

\[
\max_k\frac{I^\star(R,k)}{ak+cR}
\]

를 계산하면 전역 최적해를 얻습니다.

가능한 상태 수는 대략

\[
N\times R\times K_{\max}\times2
\]

이므로 시간복잡도는

\[
\boxed{O(NRK_{\max})}
\]

입니다. 여기서

\[
K_{\max}=\min(R,N-R+1)
\]

이므로 최악에는 \(O(N^3)\)이지만, \(O(2^N)\)은 아닙니다.

정확히 말하면:

\[
\boxed{
\text{청크 수의 국소적 표현}
\;+\;
\text{additive importance}
\;+\;
\widehat L(M)=aK(M)+cR(M)
\;\Longrightarrow\;
\text{정확한 다항시간 DP}
}
\]

입니다. 따라서 이 시점에서 **현재 정의한 affine fixed-\(R\) 문제의 지수 시간은 완전히 제거할 수 있다고 봐도 됩니다.** 다만 위 식으로 청크 수를 빠르게 계산할 수 있다는 사실만 발견하고 여전히 모든 마스크를 열거한다면, 탐색은 여전히 \(2^N\)입니다. 핵심은 이 국소성을 이용해 여러 마스크를 동일한 DP 상태로 합치는 것입니다.

For notational convenience, index the channel importances by `v1,…,vN`. Define

```math
Fis(r,k)
```

as the maximum importance obtainable from the first `i` channels when

- exactly `r` channels have been selected;
- exactly `k` chunks have been formed;
- channel `i` has selection state `s∈{0,1}`.

The initial conditions are

```math
F00(0,0)=0,
```

with every other initial state set to `−∞`.

If channel `i` is not selected, the previous sequence may end in either state:

```math
Fi0(r,k)=max⁡{Fi−10(r,k),Fi−11(r,k)}.
```

If channel `i` is selected, it either continues an existing chunk or starts a new one:

```math
Fi1(r,k)=vi+max⁡{Fi−11(r−1,k),Fi−10(r−1,k−1)}.
```

The maximum importance achievable with exactly `R` selected rows and `k` chunks is therefore

```math
I⋆(R,k)=max⁡s∈{0,1}FNs(R,k).


이게 어떻게 정의되길래

i1(r,k)=vi+max⁡{Fi−11(r−1,k),Fi−10(r−1,k−1)}.

이거인 거냐
```

핵심은 \(F_i^1(r,k)\)가 다음 상태를 뜻하기 때문입니다.

\[
F_i^1(r,k)
=
\begin{array}{l}
\text{앞의 \(i\)개 채널에서 정확히 \(r\)개를 선택하고,}\\
\text{정확히 \(k\)개의 청크를 만들었으며, \(M_i=1\)인 경우의 최대 importance}
\end{array}
\]

현재 채널 \(i\)는 선택되었으므로 다음 두 사실이 확정됩니다.

1. \(i\)번째 채널이 importance \(v_i\)를 추가한다.
2. 이전 \(i-1\)개 채널에서는 \(r-1\)개가 선택되어 있어야 한다.

따라서 기본적으로

\[
F_i^1(r,k)=v_i+\text{이전 상태의 최대 importance}
\]

가 됩니다.

이제 이전 채널 \(M_{i-1}\)이 무엇이었는지에 따라 두 경우로 나뉩니다.

### 경우 1: 이전 채널도 선택됨

\[
M_{i-1}=1,\qquad M_i=1
\]

즉, `1→1`이므로 \(i\)번째 채널은 기존 청크를 연장합니다.

```text
... 0 1 1 1
          ↑ i
```

새 청크가 생기지 않으므로 이전에도 이미 청크가 \(k\)개였습니다. 이전 상태는

\[
F_{i-1}^1(r-1,k)
\]

입니다.

### 경우 2: 이전 채널은 선택되지 않음

\[
M_{i-1}=0,\qquad M_i=1
\]

즉, `0→1`이므로 \(i\)번째 채널에서 새로운 청크가 시작됩니다.

```text
... 1 0 1
          ↑ i
```

현재 새 청크 하나가 추가되어 최종 청크 수가 \(k\)가 되었으므로, 이전에는 청크가 \(k-1\)개여야 합니다. 이전 상태는

\[
F_{i-1}^0(r-1,k-1)
\]

입니다.

두 경우 중 importance가 더 큰 것을 선택하고 현재 채널의 \(v_i\)를 더하면

\[
\boxed{
F_i^1(r,k)
=
v_i+
\max
\left\{
F_{i-1}^1(r-1,k),
F_{i-1}^0(r-1,k-1)
\right\}.
}
\]

정리하면:

| 이전 상태 | 전환 | 의미 | 이전 청크 수 |
|---|---|---|---:|
| \(M_{i-1}=1\) | `1→1` | 기존 청크 연장 | \(k\) |
| \(M_{i-1}=0\) | `0→1` | 새 청크 시작 | \(k-1\) |

예를 들어 \(F_i^1(4,2)\)를 구한다고 합시다. 이는 \(i\)번째 채널까지 총 4개를 선택하고 2개의 청크를 만들며 \(i\)번째 채널은 선택된 상태입니다. 가능한 이전 상태는 정확히 두 가지입니다.

\[
F_{i-1}^1(3,2)
\]

에서 기존 두 번째 청크를 연장하거나,

\[
F_{i-1}^0(3,1)
\]

에서 두 번째 청크를 새로 시작하는 것입니다. 따라서

\[
F_i^1(4,2)
=
v_i+
\max\left\{
F_{i-1}^1(3,2),
F_{i-1}^0(3,1)
\right\}.
\]

첫 번째 채널을 선택하는 경우도 같은 식으로 처리됩니다.

\[
F_1^1(1,1)
=
v_1+
F_0^0(0,0)
=
v_1.
\]

즉, 빈 수열의 마지막 상태를 \(0\)으로 간주하면 첫 번째 `1`도 `0→1`, 곧 새로운 청크의 시작으로 자연스럽게 처리됩니다.

잠깐. 

우리가 실제로 고려하는 chunk의 갯수는 얼마냐 논문에서 정확히

왜냐면 딱 그정도의 시간은 소모해도 되자나

여기서는 세 가지 “청크 개수”를 구분해야 합니다.

| 기호/수량 | 의미 |
|---|---|
| \(K(M)\) | 최종 마스크에 생긴 maximal chunk 수 |
| \(|\mathcal G|\) | 논문 알고리즘이 생성하여 점수를 계산·정렬하는 candidate chunk 수 |
| \(K_{\max}\) | 우리 exact DP가 고려해야 하는 가능한 \(K(M)\)의 최댓값 |

결론부터 말하면, **논문은 최종 \(K(M)\)을 특정 값으로 고정하지 않으며, 그 개수도 직접 보고하지 않습니다.** 논문이 직접 제한하는 것은 계산 시간이어서, 행렬 하나당 선택 오버헤드를 약 \(2\) ms 이하로 맞춥니다.

### 논문이 실제로 검사하는 candidate 수

논문 알고리즘에서 후보 길이 집합을 \(\mathcal S\), stride cap을 \(j\)라고 하면 후보 수는

\[
|\mathcal G|
=
\sum_{r\in\mathcal S}
\left(
\left\lfloor
\frac{N-r}{\min(r,j)}
\right\rfloor+1
\right)
\]

입니다.

Appendix E의 알고리즘과 Appendix H의 하이퍼파라미터를 FP16 행 크기에 적용하면, LLaVA-OneVision-7B의 후보 수는 대략 다음과 같습니다.

| Weight shape | AGX 후보 수 | Nano 후보 수 |
|---|---:|---:|
| \(3584\times3584\) | 28,552 | 11,874 |
| \(3584\times512\) | 8,540 | 12,390 |
| \(3584\times18944\) | 21,489 | 32,220 |
| \(18944\times3584\) | 37,860 | 34,056 |

이 숫자는 논문이 표에 직접 기재한 값이 아니라, Algorithm 1과 Table 2로부터 계산한 값입니다. 전체 Table 2의 행렬들을 기준으로 하면 대략

\[
\boxed{406\text{개}\;\sim\;49{,}086\text{개}}
\]

의 candidate chunk를 점수화합니다. 그리고 이를 GPU에서 정렬하는 과정이 선택 시간의 \(80\%\) 이상을 차지한다고 논문은 설명합니다.

### 그런데 이것은 DP의 \(k\)와 다르다

우리 DP의 \(k\)는 후보 개수가 아니라 최종 마스크의 maximal chunk 수입니다.

정확한 fixed-\(R\) 문제에서 가능한 최댓값은

\[
\boxed{
K_{\max}=\min(R,N-R+1)
}
\]

입니다.

왜냐하면:

- 청크 하나당 적어도 선택 채널 하나가 필요하므로 \(K(M)\le R\)
- 서로 다른 청크 사이에는 적어도 하나의 `0`이 필요하므로 \(K(M)\le N-R+1\)

예를 들어 \(N=18{,}944\)이고, 논문의 overhead 측정처럼 sparsity가 \(0.1\)이면

\[
R=\lfloor0.9N\rfloor=17{,}049
\]

이고

\[
K_{\max}
=
\min(17{,}049,18{,}944-17{,}049+1)
=
1{,}896.
\]

따라서 직접 DP의 상태 수는 대략

\[
NRK_{\max}
=
18{,}944\times17{,}049\times1{,}896
\approx6.12\times10^{11}
\]

입니다. 이는 논문이 정렬하는 약 \(3.8\times10^4\)개의 후보보다 압도적으로 큽니다.

\(k\) 차원을 제거하는 parametric DP를 사용해도 한 번의 실행이

\[
NR
\approx
18{,}944\times17{,}049
\approx3.23\times10^8
\]

상태이므로, 현재 형태 그대로는 논문의 \(2\) ms 알고리즘과 경쟁하기 어렵습니다.

따라서 정확한 결론은 다음과 같습니다.

\[
\boxed{
2^N\text{은 다항시간 DP로 제거했지만, }
\text{그 DP가 곧바로 실시간으로 빠른 것은 아니다.}
}
\]

논문과 공정하게 경쟁할 때 맞춰야 할 기준은 candidate 개수가 아니라

\[
\boxed{\text{행렬당 선택 시간 }<2\text{ ms}}
\]

입니다. 논문의 최종 \(K(M)\)가 작아 보인다는 이유로 DP의 \(k\) 범위를 임의로 줄이면 빨라질 수 있지만, 그 순간 전역 최적성 보장은 사라집니다. 따라서 다음 연구 문제는 **chain 구조를 더 강하게 이용해 \(O(NR)\)보다 빠른 exact 또는 근사 알고리즘을 만드는 것**입니다.
